4
Robust State-Space Collapse

Solutions to the fluid model equations (
2.1
)-(
2.7
) are Lipschitz continuous, hence almost everywhere differentiable. Following standard terminology, we refer to times
t
where the solution is differentiable as “regular.” For a static priority policy
\pi
, we use
\mathcal{L}_{\pi}
to denote the lowest priority class in each station, and
\mathcal{H}_{\pi}=\mathcal{K}\backslash\mathcal{L}_{\pi}
. To make explicit the connection between a static-priority policy ratio matrix
\Delta
and the high-priority classes, we sometimes write
\mathcal{H}_{\pi}^{\Delta}
.

The main theorem of this section relates state-space collapse for arbitrary queue-ratio policies to the linear attraction of static-priority policies. In the fluid model of static-priority policies, only the identity of the lowest-priority class matters; the ordering among the high-priority classes does not.

Definition 8

(Linear Attraction)

We say that linear attraction holds for the static-priority policy with ratio matrix
\Delta
if there exists a test vector
h\in\mathbb{R}^{K}_{++}
and a constant
r>0
such that any solution to the fluid model equations (
2.1
)-(
2.7
) satisfies

\sum_{k\in\mathcal{H}_{\pi}^{\Delta}:\overline{Z}_{k}>0}h_{k}\dot{\overline{Z}}_{k}(t)\leq-r\text{ for any regular time t with }||\overline{Z}_{\mathcal{H}_{\pi}^{\Delta}}(t)||>0.

Theorem 7

Suppose that linear attraction holds for all static priority
\Delta
with a common test vector
h\in\mathbb{R}^{K}_{++}
and a constant
r>0
. Then, for any ratio matrix
\Delta
and any initial condition
\overline{Z}(0)
, there exists a
t_{0}
such that
\overline{\epsilon}(t)=\overline{Z}(t)-\Delta CM\overline{Z}(t)=0
for all
t\geq t_{0}
.

Theorem
7
stipulates that queue-ratio SSC is inherited from the static priority policies: SSC of all static priority policies implies SSC for any queue-ratio policy. Intuitively, a queue-ratio policy moves between static priority policies, depending on the balance status of the queues: the classes in the set
\{\ell\in\mathcal{C}(j):Z_{\ell}(t)-\delta_{\ell}W_{j}(t)>0\}
are prioritized at time
t
over all others. The proof of Theorem
7
(in §
5
) proceeds by showing that the set of possible QR actions at a given state of the queues is the same as that set for
some
priority permutation and then leveraging the assumed linear attraction of all priority policies.

4.1
Example 1: The DHV Network

The detailed analysis of linear attraction for the 8 static-priority policies appears in Appendix
B.2
and establishes that the requirements of Theorem
7
hold for the balanced DHV network if and only if there exists a vector
h
that satisfies the following inequalities:

\displaystyle h_{1},h_{2},h_{3},h_{4},h_{5},h_{6}>0

(4.1)

\displaystyle h_{2}(\mu_{1}-\mu_{2})-h_{6}\mu_{6}<0

(4.2)

\displaystyle h_{4}(\mu_{3}-\mu_{4})-h_{2}\mu_{2}<0

(4.3)

\displaystyle h_{6}(\mu_{5}-\mu_{6})-h_{4}\mu_{4}<0

(4.4)

\displaystyle h_{1}(\alpha_{1}-\mu_{1})+h_{2}(\mu_{1}-\mu_{2})+h_{3}(\mu_{2}-\mu_{3})<0

(4.5)

\displaystyle h_{1}(\alpha_{1}-\mu_{1})+h_{2}(\mu_{1}-\mu_{2})<0

(4.6)

\displaystyle h_{1}(\alpha_{1}-\mu_{1})+h_{3}(\mu_{1}-\mu_{3})<0

(4.7)

\displaystyle h_{2}(\alpha_{1}-\mu_{2})+h_{3}(\mu_{2}-\mu_{3})<0

(4.8)

\displaystyle h_{3}(\mu_{2}-\mu_{3})+h_{4}(\mu_{3}-\mu_{4})-h_{2}\mu_{2}<0

(4.9)

\displaystyle h_{2}\left[\mu_{1}\left(1-\frac{\mu_{3}}{\mu_{4}}\right)-\mu_{2}\right]+h_{3}(\mu_{2}-\mu_{3})<0

(4.10)

\displaystyle h_{4}(\mu_{2}-\mu_{4})-h_{2}\mu_{2}<0

(4.11)

\displaystyle h_{4}(\mu_{3}-\mu_{4})-h_{3}\mu_{3}<0

(4.12)

\displaystyle h_{4}(\mu_{3}-\mu_{4})+h_{5}(\mu_{4}-\mu_{5})-h_{3}\mu_{3}<0

(4.13)

\displaystyle h_{3}\left[\mu_{2}\left(1-\frac{\mu_{4}}{\mu_{5}}\right)-\mu_{3}\right]+h_{4}(\mu_{3}-\mu_{4})<0

(4.14)

\displaystyle h_{5}(\mu_{3}-\mu_{5})-h_{3}\mu_{3}<0

(4.15)

\displaystyle h_{5}(\mu_{4}-\mu_{5})-h_{4}\mu_{4}<0

(4.16)

\displaystyle h_{5}(\mu_{4}-\mu_{5})+h_{6}(\mu_{5}-\mu_{6})-h_{4}\mu_{4}<0

(4.17)

\displaystyle h_{4}\left[\mu_{3}\left(1-\frac{\mu_{5}}{\mu_{6}}\right)-\mu_{4}\right]+h_{5}(\mu_{4}-\mu_{5})<0

(4.18)

\displaystyle h_{6}(\mu_{4}-\mu_{6})-h_{4}\mu_{4}<0

(4.19)

\displaystyle h_{6}(\mu_{5}-\mu_{6})-h_{5}\mu_{5}<0

(4.20)

\displaystyle h_{1}(\alpha_{1}-\mu_{1})+h_{6}(\mu_{5}-\mu_{6})-h_{5}\mu_{5}<0

(4.21)

\displaystyle h_{5}\left[\mu_{4}\left(1-\frac{\alpha_{1}}{\mu_{1}}\right)-\mu_{5}\right]+h_{6}(\mu_{5}-\mu_{6})<0

(4.22)

\displaystyle h_{1}(\alpha_{1}-\mu_{1})+h_{2}(\mu_{1}-\mu_{2})-h_{6}\mu_{6}<0

(4.23)

\displaystyle h_{1}(\alpha_{1}-\mu_{1})+h_{2}(\mu_{1}-\mu_{2})<0

(4.24)

\displaystyle h_{1}(\alpha_{1}-\mu_{1})+h_{3}(\mu_{2}-\mu_{3})<0.

(4.25)

Exactly how these inequalities arise from the eight priority policies is made clear in the appendices. Equation (
5.15
) in the proof of Theorem
7
grounds the general “recipe” for producing these inequalities. We note that inequality (
4.23
) is immediately satisfied if (
4.6
) is satisfied; and inequality (
4.24
) is the same as (
4.6
); 23 inequalities remain.

The analysis of these inequalities in Appendix
B.3
establishes the following.

Corollary 4

Consider the balanced DHV network. There exists
\bar{\alpha}\in(0,1)
(dependent on
M
) such that if
\alpha_{1}>\bar{\alpha}
and

(i)

the nominal-load conditions are satisfied:
\rho_{1}=\rho_{2}=\rho_{3}<1
(equivalently,
\alpha_{1}<1
),

(ii)

m_{2}+m_{4}+m_{6}<2
,

then the network satisfies SSC under any queue-ratio policy.

The exact value of
\bar{\alpha}
appears in Appendix
B.2
. The requirement that
\alpha
is “large enough” is for SSC only—it is not required for SP Stability—and arises from our insistence on using linear attraction for SSC. In the absence of this requirement, some high priority queues grow before decreasing, a behavior that is inconsistent with linear Lyapunov attraction. It is plausible that this requirement can be relaxed; see further discussion in §
6
. Importantly, the requirement that
\alpha_{1}>\bar{\alpha}
, is immediately satisfied if the network is in heavy traffic.

Combining Corollary
4
with the robust SP stability in Corollary
1
, we conclude the following.

Corollary 5

Consider the balanced DHV network. There exists
\bar{\alpha}\in(0,1)
(dependent on
M
) such that if
\alpha_{1}>\bar{\alpha}
and

(i)

the nominal-load conditions are satisfied:
\rho_{1}=\rho_{2}=\rho_{3}<1
(equivalently,
\alpha_{1}<1
),

(ii)

m_{2}+m_{4}+m_{6}<2
,

then the network
is robustly queue-ratio stable.

The condition
m_{2}+m_{4}+m_{6}<2
is weaker than the sufficient global-stability condition
m_{2}+m_{4}m_{6}<1
in
[
11
]
. The gap between our condition and the one in
[
11
]
is consistent with our focus on queue-ratio policies. The policies considered in
[
11
]
are general non-idling policies, of which the family of queue-ratio policies is a subset.

4.2
Example 2: The Push-Started Lu-Kumar Network

In this network (see Figure
7
), three classes are served at station 1, so we must specify the choice of the class for service when the set
\{\ell\in\mathcal{C}(j):Z_{l}(t)-\delta_{l}W_{j}(t)>0\}
contains more than one class. We fix the priority order to
1>3>4
as the “tie-breaking” rule: if both queue 1 and queue 3 are above their target, class 1 has priority over class 3.

The detailed analysis of linear attraction for the 6 static-priority policies appears in Appendix
C.2
and establishes that the requirements of Theorem
7
hold for this network if and only if there exists a vector
h
that satisfies the following inequalities:

\displaystyle h_{1},h_{2},h_{3},h_{4},h_{5}>0

(4.26)

\displaystyle-h_{4}\mu_{4}+h_{5}(\mu_{4}-\mu_{5})<0

(4.27)

\displaystyle h_{3}(\mu_{2}-\mu_{3})+h_{4}\mu_{3}<0

(4.28)

\displaystyle-h_{2}\mu_{2}+h_{3}(\mu_{2}-\mu_{3})+h_{4}\mu_{3}<0

(4.29)

\displaystyle-h_{3}\mu_{3}+h_{4}\mu_{3}<0

(4.30)

\displaystyle h_{1}(\alpha_{1}-\mu_{1})+h_{2}(\mu_{1}-\mu_{2})<0

(4.31)

\displaystyle h_{1}(\alpha_{1}-\mu_{1})+h_{3}\mu_{2}<0

(4.32)

\displaystyle m_{2}m_{4}>m_{4}m_{5}

(4.33)

\displaystyle h_{1}(\alpha_{1}-\mu_{1})+h_{2}(\mu_{1}-\mu_{2})+h_{3}\mu_{2}<0

(4.34)

\displaystyle h_{1}(\alpha_{1}-\mu_{1})+h_{3}\mu_{1}<0\;\;\text{if}\;\;\mu_{1}\leq\mu_{2}.

(4.35)

These arise from the different priority orders. Equation (
4.33
), specifically, arises from the priority order
\pi\{1,3,5\}
. Inequalities (
4.29
) and (
4.30
) are immediately satisfied if (
4.27
) is. The inequality (
4.31
) is satisfied if (
4.34
) is, and (
4.35
) is satisfied if (
4.32
) is. Thus, we need only consider the inequality
m_{2}m_{4}>m_{3}m_{5}
and the remaining five inequalities:

\left\{\begin{array}[]{lr}h_{1},h_{2},h_{3},h_{4},h_{5}>0\\
-h_{4}\mu_{4}+h_{5}(\mu_{4}-\mu_{5})<0\\
h_{3}(\mu_{2}-\mu_{3})+h_{4}\mu_{3}<0\\
h_{1}(\alpha_{1}-\mu_{1})+h_{3}\mu_{2}<0\\
h_{1}(\alpha_{1}-\mu_{1})+h_{2}(\mu_{1}-\mu_{2})+h_{3}\mu_{2}<0.\\
\end{array}\right.

We make two useful observations: (i) If
\mu_{2}\geq\mu_{3}
(
m_{2}\leq m_{3}
), the inequality
h_{3}(\mu_{2}-\mu_{3})+h_{4}\mu_{3}<0
cannot be satisfied with positive
h_{3}
and
h_{4}
. When
m_{2}>m_{3}
, in contrast, there exists a positive vector
h
with
h_{1}\geq h_{3}\geq h_{4}\geq h_{5}
and
h_{1}\geq h_{2}
satisfying the five inequalities. Thus,
m_{2}>m_{3}
is a sufficient condition for the existence of an
h
that satisfies the five inequalities; and (ii) for the balanced network
m_{2}m_{4}>m_{3}m_{5}
implies
m_{2}<m_{3}
. This is because, with
m_{1}+m_{3}+m_{4}=m_{2}+m_{5}=1
,
m_{2}m_{4}>m_{3}m_{5}\Leftrightarrow m_{2}m_{4}>m_{3}(1-m_{2})\Leftrightarrow m_{2}(m_{3}+m_{4})>m_{3}\Leftrightarrow m_{2}(1-m_{1})>m_{3}\Rightarrow m_{2}>m_{3}
.

Combining these observations, we conclude that
m_{2}m_{4}>m_{3}m_{5}
suffices for the existence of a strictly positive vector
h
that satisfies all the inequalities. Theorem
7
, then yields the following corollary. Below, the tie-breaking rule is
1>3>4
.

Corollary 6

Consider the balanced push-started Lu-Kumar network. There exists
\bar{\alpha}\in(0,1)
(dependent on
M
) such that if
\alpha_{1}>\bar{\alpha}
, and

(i)

the nominal-load condition is satisfied:
\rho_{1}=\rho_{2}<1
(equivalently,
\alpha_{1}<1
),

(ii)

m_{2}m_{4}>m_{3}m_{5}
,

then the network satisfies SSC under any queue-ratio policy.

Combining this with Corollary
2
, we conclude the following.

Corollary 7

Consider the balanced push-started Lu-Kumar network. There exists
\bar{\alpha}\in(0,1)
(dependent on
M
) such that if
\alpha_{1}>\bar{\alpha}
, and

(i)

the nominal-load condition is satisfied:
\rho_{1}=\rho_{2}<1
(equivalently,
\alpha_{1}<1
),

(ii)

m_{2}m_{4}>m_{3}m_{5}
,

then the network is robustly queue-ratio stable.

With load balance, the requirement
m_{2}m_{4}>m_{3}m_{5}
is equivalent to the requirement
\frac{m_{3}}{1-m_{1}}+m_{5}<1
. The latter is the sufficient condition for global stability in
[
12
]
. This is because, in this case,
m_{2}m_{4}>m_{3}m_{5}\Leftrightarrow(1-m_{5})(1-m_{1}-m_{3})>m_{3}m_{5}\Leftrightarrow 1-m_{1}>m_{3}+m_{5}-m_{1}m_{5}\Leftrightarrow 1-m_{1}>m_{3}+m_{5}(1-m_{1})\Leftrightarrow\frac{m_{3}}{1-m_{1}}+m_{5}<1
. This last condition, known already from
[
12
]
, is intuitively understood by considering that, once server 1 allocates capacity to class 1, the residual network is a Lu-Kumar network where the global stability condition would be
m_{3}+m_{5}<1
. Since part of server 1’s time is dedicated to class 1, we essentially “rescale” the average service time for class 3 by
1/({1-m_{1})}
to obtain a modified version of this inequality:
\frac{m_{3}}{1-m_{1}}+m_{5}<1
.

4.3
Example 3: The Lu-Kumar Network

The detailed analysis of linear attraction for the 4 static-priority policies appears in Appendix
D.2
and establishes that the requirements of Theorem
7
hold for the this network if and only if
m_{2}+m_{4}<1
.

Corollary 8

A balanced Lu-Kumar network satisfies SSC under any queue-ratio policy if

(i)

the nominal-load condition is satisfied:
\rho_{1}=\rho_{2}<1
(equivalently,
\alpha_{1}<1
);

(ii)

m_{2}+m_{4}<1
.

Combining this with Corollary
3
, we conclude the following.

Corollary 9

A balanced Lu-Kumar network is robustly queue-ratio stable if

(i)

the nominal-load condition is satisfied:
\rho_{1}=\rho_{2}<1
(equivalently,
\alpha_{1}<1
);

(ii)

m_{2}+m_{4}<1
.

The requirement
m_{2}+m_{4}<1
is necessary and sufficient for global stability; see
[
15
]
.

5
Proofs of Theorems

For the proof of Theorem
6
, we will need Lemma
4
below. Suppose
\Delta_{1}
and
\Delta_{2}
are different only in the
k^{th}
column,
R_{\Delta_{3}}^{-1}=CMQ(\lambda\Delta_{1}+(1-\lambda)\Delta_{2})=CMQ\Delta_{1}+(1-\lambda)CMQ(\Delta_{2}-\Delta_{1})=R_{\Delta_{1}}^{-1}+(1-\lambda)CMQ(\Delta_{2}-\Delta_{1})
, where
CMQ(\Delta_{2}-\Delta_{1})
is a matrix with only one non-zero column, say the
k^{th}
column. We can rewrite
CMQ(\Delta_{2}-\Delta_{1})
as

\displaystyle uv^{\top}=CMQ(\Delta_{2}-\Delta_{1}),\text{where}

(5.1)

u
is a column vector whose elements are the same as the
k^{th}
column in
CMQ(\Delta_{2}-\Delta_{1})
,

(5.2)

v
is a column vector with the
k^{th}
element 1 and all other elements 0.

(5.3)

Thus, we have

R_{\Delta_{3}}^{-1}=R_{\Delta_{1}}^{-1}+(1-\lambda)CMQ(\Delta_{2}-\Delta_{1})=R_{\Delta_{1}}^{-1}+(1-\lambda)uv^{\top}

(5.4)

Similarly,

R_{\Delta_{3}}^{-1}=CMQ\Delta_{2}+\lambda CMQ(\Delta_{1}-\Delta_{2})=R_{\Delta_{2}}^{-1}-\lambda CMQ(\Delta_{2}-\Delta_{1})=R_{\Delta_{2}}^{-1}-\lambda uv^{\top}.

(5.5)

Lemma 4

Fix network primitives
(C,M,P,\alpha)
and two ratio-matrix neighbors
\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2}
that differ only in the
k^{th}
column and a third ratio matrix
\Delta_{3}=\lambda\Delta_{1}+(1-\lambda)\Delta_{2}
,
\lambda\in(0,1)
. Then,
\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2},\Delta_{3}
are ratio-matrix neighbors. Assume that the reflection matrices
R_{\Delta_{i}}=(CMQ\Delta_{i})^{-1},i=1,2,3
are invertible, and let
u,v
be the vectors that satisfy (
5.1
)-(
5.3
) for
\Delta_{1}
and
\Delta_{2}
. Then,

R_{\Delta_{1}}
and
R_{\Delta_{2}}
are proportional in the
k^{th}
row with the ratio

(R_{\Delta_{1}})_{k.}=\frac{det(R_{\Delta_{2}}^{-1})}{det(R_{\Delta_{1}}^{-1})}(R_{\Delta_{2}})_{k.},

(5.6)

and
R_{\Delta_{3}}=R_{\Delta_{1}}(I-(1-\lambda)A)=R_{\Delta_{2}}(I+\lambda A)
, where

A=\frac{uv^{\top}R_{\Delta_{1}}}{1+(1-\lambda)v^{\top}R_{\Delta_{1}}u}=\frac{uv^{\top}R_{\Delta_{2}}}{1-\lambda v^{\top}R_{\Delta_{2}}u}.

Proof.

Proof of Theorem
6
.

By Lemma
4
,

R_{\Delta_{3}}=R_{\Delta_{1}}(I-(1-\lambda)A)=R_{\Delta_{2}}(I+\lambda A),

(5.7)

where, recall,

A=\frac{uv^{\top}R_{\Delta_{1}}}{1+(1-\lambda)v^{\top}R_{\Delta_{1}}u}=\frac{uv^{\top}R_{\Delta_{2}}}{1-\lambda v^{\top}R_{\Delta_{2}}u}.

Defining

\beta_{\lambda}=1-\frac{1-\lambda}{1-\lambda v^{\top}R_{\Delta_{2}}u},

we have

\begin{split}A&=\frac{1-\beta_{\lambda}}{1-\lambda}uv^{\top}R_{\Delta_{2}}=\frac{1-\beta_{\lambda}}{1-\lambda}(R_{\Delta_{2}}^{-1}-R_{\Delta_{1}}^{-1})R_{\Delta_{2}}=\frac{1-\beta_{\lambda}}{1-\lambda}(I-R_{\Delta_{1}}^{-1}R_{\Delta_{2}}),\end{split}

so that

\begin{split}R_{\Delta_{3}}&=R_{\Delta_{1}}(I-(1-\lambda)A)=R_{\Delta_{1}}(I-(1-\beta_{\lambda})(I-R_{\Delta_{1}}^{-1}R_{\Delta_{2}}))\\
&=R_{\Delta_{1}}-(1-\beta_{\lambda})R_{\Delta_{1}}+(1-\beta_{\lambda})R_{\Delta_{1}}R_{\Delta_{1}}^{-1}R_{\Delta_{2}}=\beta_{\lambda}R_{\Delta_{1}}+(1-\beta_{\lambda})R_{\Delta_{2}}.\end{split}

Since

1-\beta_{\lambda}=\frac{1-\lambda}{1-\lambda v^{\top}R_{\Delta_{2}}u},\;A=\frac{1-\beta_{\lambda}}{1-\lambda}(I-R_{\Delta_{1}}^{-1}R_{\Delta_{2}}),

we have

R_{\Delta_{1}}A=\frac{1-\beta_{\lambda}}{1-\lambda}(R_{\Delta_{1}}-R_{\Delta_{2}}).

(5.8)

By (
5.7
) we have

\lambda R_{\Delta_{2}}A=R_{\Delta_{3}}-R_{\Delta_{2}}=\beta_{\lambda}R_{\Delta_{1}}+(1-\beta_{\lambda})R_{\Delta_{2}}-R_{\Delta_{2}}=\beta_{\lambda}(R_{\Delta_{1}}-R_{\Delta_{2}}),

so that

R_{\Delta_{2}}A=\frac{\beta_{\lambda}}{\lambda}(R_{\Delta_{1}}-R_{\Delta_{2}}).

(5.9)

Consequently, from (
5.8
) and (
5.9
),

\frac{R_{\Delta_{1}}A}{R_{\Delta_{2}}A}=\frac{(1-\beta_{\lambda})\lambda}{(1-\lambda)\beta_{\lambda}}.

(5.10)

Since

\beta_{\lambda}=1-\frac{1-\lambda}{1-\lambda v^{\top}R_{\Delta_{2}}u}=\frac{\lambda(1-v^{\top}R_{\Delta_{2}}u)}{1-\lambda v^{\top}R_{\Delta_{2}}u},

we have

\begin{split}\frac{R_{\Delta_{1}}A}{R_{\Delta_{2}}A}&=\frac{(1-\beta_{\lambda})\lambda}{(1-\lambda)\beta_{\lambda}}=\frac{\lambda}{1-\lambda}\frac{\displaystyle\frac{1-\lambda}{1-\lambda v^{\top}R_{\Delta_{2}}u}}{\displaystyle\frac{\lambda(1-v^{\top}R_{\Delta_{2}}u)}{1-\lambda v^{\top}R_{\Delta_{2}}u}}=\frac{1}{1-v^{\top}R_{\Delta_{2}}u}=\frac{1}{1-(R_{\Delta_{2}})_{k.}((R_{\Delta_{2}}^{-1})_{.k}-(R_{\Delta_{1}}^{-1})_{.k})}\\
&=\frac{1}{(R_{\Delta_{2}})_{k.}(R_{\Delta_{1}}^{-1})_{.k}}=\frac{det(R_{\Delta_{2}}^{-1})}{det(R_{\Delta_{1}}^{-1})(R_{\Delta_{1}})_{k.}(R_{\Delta_{1}}^{-1})_{.k}}=\frac{det(R_{\Delta_{2}}^{-1})}{det(R_{\Delta_{1}}^{-1})}=\frac{det(R_{\Delta_{1}})}{det(R_{\Delta_{2}})},\end{split}

where the fourth equation follows from
v^{\top}R_{\Delta_{2}}=(R_{\Delta_{2}})_{k.}
,
u=(R_{\Delta_{2}}^{-1})_{.k}-(R_{\Delta_{1}}^{-1})_{.k}
, and the sixth equation follows (
5.6
).

Thus, from (
5.10
),

\frac{1-\beta_{\lambda}}{\beta_{\lambda}}=\frac{1-\lambda}{\lambda}\frac{det(R_{\Delta_{1}})}{det(R_{\Delta_{2}})}\;\Rightarrow\;\beta_{\lambda}=\frac{\lambda det(R_{\Delta_{2}})}{\lambda det(R_{\Delta_{2}})+(1-\lambda)det(R_{\Delta_{1}})}.

Because

\frac{\partial\beta_{\lambda}}{\partial\lambda}=\frac{det(R_{\Delta_{2}})det(R_{\Delta_{1}})}{(\lambda det(R_{\Delta_{2}})+(1-\lambda)det(R_{\Delta_{1}}))^{2}},

and its sign is determined by
det(R_{\Delta_{2}})det(R_{\Delta_{1}})
,
\beta_{\lambda}
is monotone in
\lambda
. Since
\beta_{\lambda=0}=0
,
\beta_{\lambda=1}=1
, we conclude that
\beta_{\lambda}\in(0,1)
. In the analysis below
\lambda
is fixed, so to simplify notation, we define
\beta=\beta_{\lambda}
.

Since
\Delta_{1}
and
\Delta_{2}
are ratio-matrix neighbors as defined in Definition
7
and differ only in the
k^{th}
column, the matrices
R_{\Delta_{1}}^{-1}=CMQ\Delta_{1}
,
R_{\Delta_{2}}^{-1}=CMQ\Delta_{2}
,
R_{\Delta_{3}}^{-1}=\lambda R_{\Delta_{1}}^{-1}+(1-\lambda)R_{\Delta_{2}}^{-1}
as well differ only in the
k^{th}
column, and

\begin{split}&det(R_{\Delta_{3}}^{-1})=\lambda det(R_{\Delta_{1}}^{-1})+(1-\lambda)det(R_{\Delta_{1}}^{-1})\Leftrightarrow\;\;\frac{1}{det(R_{\Delta_{3}})}=\frac{\lambda}{det(R_{\Delta_{1}})}+\frac{1-\lambda}{det(R_{\Delta_{2}})}\\
\Leftrightarrow\;\;&\lambda det(R_{\Delta_{2}})+(1-\lambda)det(R_{\Delta_{1}})=\frac{det(R_{\Delta_{2}})det(R_{\Delta_{1}})}{det(R_{\Delta_{3}})}.\end{split}

Thus, we have

\beta=\displaystyle\frac{\lambda det(R_{\Delta_{2}})}{\lambda det(R_{\Delta_{2}})+(1-\lambda)det(R_{\Delta_{1}})}=\frac{\lambda det(R_{\Delta_{3}})}{det(R_{\Delta_{1}})},\text{ and }1-\beta=\displaystyle\frac{(1-\lambda)det(R_{\Delta_{3}})}{det(R_{\Delta_{2}})}.

Consequently,
R_{\Delta_{3}}=\beta R_{\Delta_{1}}+(1-\beta)R_{\Delta_{2}}
can also be written as

\frac{R_{\Delta_{3}}}{det(R_{\Delta_{3}})}=\lambda\frac{R_{\Delta_{1}}}{det(R_{\Delta_{1}})}+(1-\lambda)\frac{R_{\Delta_{2}}}{det(R_{\Delta_{2}})}.

∎∎

Proof.

Proof of Theorem
5
.

We first consider the completely-
\mathcal{S}
property. For fixed
\Delta
(in turn fixed
R=(CMQ\Delta)^{-1}
), it follows from the definition of completely-
\mathcal{S}
that the property holds if the following LP has a strictly positive optimal value:

\begin{split}P(\Delta)={\max_{\epsilon,u^{b}}}\quad&\epsilon\\
s.t.\quad&R_{b}u^{b}\geq\epsilon e^{b}\qquad\forall b\subseteq J\\
&u^{b}\geq e^{b}\qquad\forall b\subseteq J\\
&\epsilon\leq 1\\
&\epsilon\in\mathbb{R},u^{b}\in\mathbb{R}^{|b|}\end{split}.

(5.11)

The constraint
\epsilon\leq 1
is added here to guarantee that the problem is bounded. Otherwise, if there exists a feasible solution
(\epsilon,u^{b})
with
\epsilon>0
, then
(\kappa\epsilon,\kappa u^{b})
is feasible for any
\kappa>0
so the problem would be unbounded.

With this constraint, if
P(\Delta)>0
, then
P(\Delta)=1
. Also notice that the LP is always feasible. Indeed,
u^{b}=e^{b}
,
\forall b\in J
and
\epsilon=\max\{x:\;x\leq 1,x\leq R_{b}e^{b},\;\forall b\subseteq J\}
form one feasible solution.

The dual of (
5.11
) is

\begin{aligned} D(\Delta)=\;&\underset{z^{b}}{\text{min}}&&1-\sum_{b\subseteq J}e^{b\top}z^{b}+\sum_{b\subseteq J}e^{b\top}(R_{\Delta})_{b}^{\top}z^{b}\\
&\text{s.t.}&&z^{b}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{|b|}\\
\end{aligned}.

(5.12)

An obvious feasible solution of (
5.12
) is
z^{b}=0
, and the corresponding objective value is 1. Thus, the dual problem is always feasible. As a result, strong duality holds.

Since
R_{\Delta_{1}}
and
R_{\Delta_{1}}
are both completely-
\mathcal{S}
,
P(\Delta_{1})=P(\Delta_{2})=1
. Strong duality then guarantees that
D(\Delta_{1})=P(\Delta_{1})=1
,
D(\Delta_{2})=P(\Delta_{2})=1
. Because
\Delta_{1}
and
\Delta_{2}
are ratio-matrix neighbors, we have by Theorem
6
, that

R_{\Delta}=\beta R_{\Delta_{1}}+(1-\beta)R_{\Delta_{2}}
for
\Delta=\lambda\Delta_{1}+(1-\lambda)\Delta_{2}
. Then,

\begin{aligned} D(\Delta)=\;&\underset{z^{b}}{\text{min}}&&\beta\left(1-\sum_{b\subseteq J}e^{b\top}z^{b}+\sum_{b\subseteq J}e^{b\top}(R_{\Delta_{1}})_{b}^{\top}z^{b}\right)+(1-\beta)\left(1-\sum_{b\subseteq J}e^{b\top}z^{b}+\sum_{b\subseteq J}e^{b\top}(R_{\Delta_{2}})_{b}^{\top}z^{b}\right)\\
&\text{s.t.}&&z^{b}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{|b|}\\
\end{aligned},

\begin{aligned} D(\Delta_{1})=\;&\underset{z^{b}}{\text{min}}&&1-\sum_{b\subseteq J}e^{b\top}z^{b}+\sum_{b\subseteq J}e^{b\top}(R_{\Delta_{1}})_{b}^{\top}z^{b}\\
&\text{s.t.}&&z^{b}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{|b|}\\
\end{aligned},\quad\begin{gathered}\begin{aligned} D(\Delta_{2})=\;&\underset{z^{b}}{\text{min}}&&1-\sum_{b\subseteq J}e^{b\top}z^{b}+\sum_{b\subseteq J}e^{b\top}(R_{\Delta_{2}})_{b}^{\top}z^{b}\\
&\text{s.t.}&&z^{b}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{|b|}\\
\end{aligned}.\end{gathered}

Because the feasible regions of
D(\Delta_{1})
,
D(\Delta_{2})
and
D(\Delta)
are all
\{z^{b}:z^{b}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{|b|}\}
,
D(\Delta)\geq\beta D(\Delta_{1})+(1-\beta)D(\Delta_{2})=\beta+(1-\beta)=1
.

Due to strong duality, we then have that
1\leq D(\Delta)=P(\Delta)\leq 1
. It must then hold that
D(\Delta)=P(\Delta)=1
, and thus the reflection matrix for
\Delta
is completely-
\mathcal{S}
.

According to Lemma
1
, for a fixed
\Delta
, Chen-
\mathcal{S}
holds if the following LP has strictly positive optimal value:

\begin{split}{\max_{h,\alpha^{b},\epsilon,\eta^{b}}}\quad\epsilon\\
s.t.\quad&h_{a}^{\top}\theta_{a}-\alpha^{b\top}\theta_{b}\leq-\epsilon\qquad\forall(a,b)\\
&h_{a}^{\top}R_{ab}\leq\alpha^{b\top}R_{b}\qquad\forall(a,b)\\
&\epsilon e\leq R_{b}\eta^{b}\qquad\forall b\subseteq J\\
&\alpha^{b}\in\mathbb{R}^{|b|},h\geq e,\epsilon\leq M,\eta^{b}\geq e^{b}\end{split}.

(5.13)

The dual LP of (
5.13
) is

\begin{aligned} &\underset{}{\text{min}}&&e^{J\top}\sum_{(a,b)}N^{a\top}(R_{ab}R_{b}^{-1}\theta_{b}-\theta_{a})x^{a}+M-\sum_{(a,b)}x^{a}-\sum_{b\subseteq J}e^{b\top}z^{b}+\sum_{b\subseteq J}e^{b\top}R_{b}^{\top}z^{b}\\
&\text{s.t.}&&x^{a}\in\mathbb{R}_{+},z^{b}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{|b|}\\
\end{aligned},

where the matrix
N^{a}
maps the column vector to its sub-vector with the indices in the set
a
.

From here, an identical argument to that for completely-
\mathcal{S}
proves that
if
(R_{\Delta_{1}},\theta_{\Delta_{1}})
and
(R_{\Delta_{2}},\theta_{\Delta_{2}})
are both Chen-
\mathcal{S}
, so is
(R_{\Delta_{3}},\theta_{\Delta_{3}})
; we omit the details.
∎∎

Proof.

Proof of Theorem
7
.

Let
G(u)=\alpha-(I-P^{\prime})M^{-1}u
,
F(u)=(I-\Delta CM)G(u)
; with this notation,
\dot{\overline{Z}}(t)=G(\dot{\overline{T}}(t))
. A solution
\overline{X}=(\overline{Z}(t),\overline{W}(t),\overline{\epsilon}(t),\overline{T}(t))
to the fluid model equations (
2.1
)-(
2.7
) satisfies, for any regular
t\geq 0
,

(\overline{Z}(t),\overline{\epsilon}(t))\in\mathbb{X},\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \dot{\overline{T}}(t)\in\mathbb{U}_{\Delta}(\overline{Z}(t),\overline{\epsilon}(t)),\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \dot{\overline{Z}}(t)\in\{G(u):u\in\mathbb{U}_{\Delta}(\overline{Z}(t),\overline{\epsilon}(t))\},

where
\mathbb{X}=\{(z,\epsilon)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{K}\times\mathbb{R}^{K}:\epsilon=z-\Delta CMz\}
is the family of possible states for
\overline{Z}
and
\overline{\epsilon}
, and

\mathbb{U}_{\Delta}(z,\epsilon)=\{u\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{K}:e-Cu\geq 0,(CMz)^{\prime}(e-Cu)=0,\epsilon_{k}^{+}(1-u_{k}^{+})=0,\mathbbm{1}\{z_{k}=0\}G_{k}(u)\ =0\text{, for all }k\in\mathcal{K}\},

is the set of possible controls
u
in state
(z,\epsilon)
. Define
\overline{Z}_{k}^{+}(t)=\sum_{i\geq k:s(i)=s(k),\overline{Z}_{i}(t)>0}\overline{Z}_{i}(t)
. For a static-priority policy
\pi
,
\epsilon_{k}^{+}=z_{k}^{+}
for all
k\in\mathcal{H}_{\pi}
, and

z_{k}^{+}=0
implies
\epsilon_{k}^{+}=0
for all
k\in\mathcal{L}_{\pi}
,
so that the set
\mathbb{U}_{\Delta}(z,\epsilon)
is, for static-priority policies, replaced by

\mathbb{U}_{\pi}(z):=\{u\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{K}:e-Cu\geq 0,(CMz)^{\prime}(e-Cu)=0,z_{k}^{+}(1-u_{k}^{+})=0,\mathbbm{1}\{z_{k}=0\}G_{k}(u)\ =0\text{, for all }k\in\mathcal{K}\}.

We note that
\mathbb{U}_{\pi}(z)
depends only on the sign of
z
, not its actual value.

Recall that
\dot{\overline{Z}}(t)\in\{G(u):u\in\mathbb{U}_{\Delta}(\overline{Z}(t),\overline{\epsilon}(t))\}
. To prove the theorem it then suffices to establish the existence of an
h\in\mathbb{R}^{K}_{++}
and a constant
r>0
such that, for any
\Delta
,

\displaystyle\sum_{k:\epsilon_{k}>0}h_{k}G_{k}(u)\leq-r,\text{ for any state }(z,\epsilon)\in\mathbb{X},\text{ with }\lVert\epsilon\rVert>0\text{ and all }u\in\mathbb{U}_{\Delta}(z,\epsilon)\text{.}

(5.14)

We then have for all regular time with
\lVert\overline{\epsilon}(t)\rVert>0
,

-r\geq\sum_{k:\overline{\epsilon}_{k}(t)>0}h_{k}\dot{\overline{Z}}_{k}(t)=\sum_{j\in\mathcal{J}}\sum_{k\in\mathcal{C}(j):\overline{\epsilon}_{k}(t)>0}h_{k}(\dot{\overline{\epsilon}}_{k}(t)+\delta_{kj}\dot{\overline{W}}_{j}(t))\text{.}

Since
\overline{W}(t)
and
\sum_{k\in\mathcal{C}(j):\overline{\epsilon}_{k}(t)>0}h_{k}\overline{\epsilon}_{k}(t)
are non-negative, there exists
t_{0}<\infty
such that for all
t\geq t_{0}
, either
\overline{W}_{j}(t)=(CM\overline{Z}(t))_{j}=0
or
\sum_{k\in\mathcal{C}(j):\overline{\epsilon}_{k}(t)>0}\overline{\epsilon}_{k}(t)=0
. In the former case, since
\overline{\epsilon}_{k}(t)\leq\overline{Z}_{k}(t)
, this implies
\overline{\epsilon}_{k}(t)=0
. Thus, in either case,
\overline{\epsilon}_{k}(t)=0
for all
t\geq t_{0}
.

We prove next that
h
and
r
satisfying (
5.14
) exist if linear attraction holds for all static priority policies with common
h
and
r
. We divide the possible values of
(z,\epsilon)\in\mathbb{R}^{K}_{+}
into three cases. In all cases, the argument proceeds by showing that the set of feasible controls
U_{\Delta}(z,\epsilon)
is the same as
\mathbb{U}_{\pi}(z,\epsilon)
for some priority permutation
\pi
and then leveraging the assumed linear attraction of all priority policies.

Case 1:
For each station
j\in\mathcal{J}
, there exists a class
i\in\mathcal{C}(j)
with
\epsilon_{i}>0
.

In this case no station is empty. Let
\mathcal{H}_{\Delta}(z,\epsilon)=\{i\in\mathcal{K}:\epsilon_{i}>0\}
. We construct a policy priority permutation
{}_{\epsilon}\pi
where the classes in
\mathcal{H}_{\Delta}(z,\epsilon)
take the highest priorities in their stations.
Then,
\delta_{i}=0
for all
i\in\mathcal{K}
with
\epsilon_{i}>0
, and
\epsilon_{i}=z_{i}-\delta_{i}(CMZ)_{s(i)}=z_{i}
. Thus, if
\epsilon_{i}>0
, then
z_{i}>0
.
Given
i\in\mathcal{H}_{\Delta}(z,\epsilon)
, there exists no
k\not\in\mathcal{H}_{\Delta}(z,\epsilon)
with
s(k)=s(i)=j
such that
{}_{\epsilon}\pi_{k}(j)\geq{{}_{\epsilon}\pi_{i}(j)}
. For each
j\in\mathcal{J}
, pick
i\in\mathcal{C}(j)
with
\epsilon_{i}<0
and assign it as the lowest priority class in station
j
. Such an
i
exists by the assumption that there exists an
i
with
\epsilon_{i}>0
for each station and the fact that
CM\epsilon=0
for any
(z,\epsilon)\in\mathbb{X}
. Then we assign the remaining classes to priorities arbitrarily. Let
\mathcal{L}_{{}_{\epsilon}\pi}
be the classes with the lowest priority in each station, and
\mathcal{H}_{{}_{\epsilon}\pi}=\mathcal{K}\backslash\mathcal{L}_{{}_{\epsilon}\pi}
.

Since
z_{i}=\epsilon_{i}-\delta_{i}(CMZ)_{s(i)}>0
for all
i
with
\epsilon_{i}>0
, with the definition of
{}_{\epsilon}\pi
, we have
\mathbb{U}_{{}_{\epsilon}\pi}(z)=\mathbb{U}_{\Delta}(z,\epsilon)
. According to
{}_{\epsilon}\pi
, we have
u_{k}=1
for
k\in\mathcal{H}_{\Delta}(z,\epsilon)
and
u_{k}=0
otherwise. In particular, by the assumption of linear attraction under static-priority policies, we have that

\displaystyle\sum_{k\in\mathcal{H}_{\Delta}(z,\epsilon)}h_{k}G_{k}(u)\leq\sum_{k\in\mathcal{H}_{{}_{\epsilon}\pi}(z):z_{k}>0}h_{k}G_{k}(u)\leq-r\text{, for all }u\in\mathbb{U}_{{}_{\epsilon}\pi}(z)=\mathbb{U}_{\Delta}(z,\epsilon)\text{.}

(5.15)

The first inequality follows because
\mathcal{H}_{\Delta}(z,\epsilon)\subseteq\mathcal{H}_{{}_{\epsilon}\pi}(z)
and
u_{k}=0
(so that
G_{k}(u)\geq 0
) for all
k\not\in\mathcal{H}_{\Delta}(z,\epsilon)
. The second inequality follows from the assumed linear attraction under static-priority policies. Thus, we have (
5.14
) in this case.

The remaining cases are when
(z,\epsilon)
are such that
j\in\mathcal{J}
exists with
\epsilon_{i}=0
for all
i\in\mathcal{C}(j)
. We assume that there is a single such station, call it station
j
. The same argument extends easily to the case of multiple such stations.

Case 2:

\epsilon_{i}=0
for all
i\in\mathcal{C}(j)
, and
z_{i}=0
for all
i\in\mathcal{C}(j)
.

In this case, since
\epsilon_{i}=z_{i}=0,\mathcal{H}_{\Delta}(z,\epsilon)=\mathcal{H}_{\Delta}(z,\epsilon)/\mathcal{C}(j)
and
\mathcal{H}_{{}_{\epsilon}\pi}(z)=\mathcal{H}_{{}_{\epsilon}\pi}(z)/\mathcal{C}(j)
, neither of the sums in (
5.15
) include
\mathcal{C}(j)
. Also, we still have
\mathbb{U}_{{}_{\epsilon}\pi}(z)=\mathbb{U}_{\Delta}(z,\epsilon)
since the constraint
\mathbbm{1}\{z_{k}=0\}G_{k}(u)=0
is active for these classes in both
\mathbb{U}_{{}_{\epsilon}\pi}(z)
and
\mathbb{U}_{\Delta}(z,\epsilon)
. Thus, the argument in Case
1
also applies.

Case 3:

\epsilon_{i}=0
for all
i\in\mathcal{C}(j)
, but
z_{i}>0
for some
i\in\mathcal{C}(j)
.

Under static priority, we have in station
j
that
\delta_{kj}=0
for all but one class, and we can use again the argument from Case 1. Thus, we assume that there are distinct classes
k,l\in\mathcal{C}(j)
for which
\delta_{k},\delta_{l}>0
. Since
\epsilon_{i}=0
for all
i\in\mathcal{C}(j)
, we have
z_{k},z_{l}>0
. To complete this case, we need two auxiliary lemmas.

Lemma 5

Consider the queue-ratio fluid network model (
2.1
)-(
2.7
). Let
t
be a regular time. If
j
is such that
\sum_{k\in\mathcal{C}(j)}\overline{\epsilon}_{k}(t)=0
, it must be the case that
\dot{\overline{\epsilon}}_{k}(t)=0
for all
k\in\mathcal{C}(j)
. In particular, at every regular time
t
, we have
\dot{T}(t)\in\tilde{\mathbb{U}}_{\Delta}(\overline{Z}(t),\overline{\epsilon}(t))
where

\tilde{\mathbb{U}}_{\Delta}(z,\epsilon):=\mathbb{U}_{\Delta}(z,\epsilon)\cap\{u\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{K}:\mathbbm{1}\{\epsilon_{k}=0,k\in\mathcal{C}(j)\}F_{k}(u)=0\text{ for all }j\in\mathcal{J}\text{ and }k\in\mathcal{C}(j)\}\text{.}

We let
z^{-j}
be the entries of the vector
z\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{K}
corresponding to entries
k\not\in\mathcal{C}(j)
. Similarly,
{}_{\epsilon}\pi^{-j}
is the restriction of
{}_{\epsilon}\pi
to stations other than station
j
.

Lemma 6

Fix
(z,\epsilon)
and let
j
be the only station such that
\epsilon_{i}=0
for all
i\in\mathcal{C}(j)
. Suppose that there exist
u\in\mathbb{U}_{\Delta}(z,\epsilon),j\in\mathcal{J}
and
k\in\mathcal{C}(j)
so that
G_{k}(u)\leq 0
,
\delta_{k}>0
and
F_{l}(u)=0
for all
l\in\mathcal{C}(j)
. Then,
G_{l}(u)\leq 0
for all
l\in\mathcal{C}(j)
. Moreover,
u
is a convex combination of vectors in
\cap_{\mathcal{Q}}\mathbb{U}_{(_{\epsilon}\pi^{-j},\pi(j))}(z^{-j},x^{j})
, where
\mathcal{Q}=\{\pi(j),x^{j}:x_{k}^{j}=0\text{ for }k\in\mathcal{H}_{(_{\epsilon}\pi^{-j},\pi(j))}\}
.

The latter part of Lemma
6
means that
u_{j}
, the allocation in station
j
, is a convex combination of allocations under static priorities in station
j
when the high-priority queues are empty.

Since
\epsilon_{i}=0
for all
i\in\mathcal{C}(j)
, Lemma
5
implies that
F_{k}(u)=0
. Let
u\in\mathbb{U}_{\Delta}(z,\epsilon)
, then
u_{k}=1
for all
k\not\in\mathcal{C}(j)
since
\epsilon_{i}>0
in each such station. (1) If
G_{k}(u)\geq 0
for all
k
with
\delta_{k}>0
and all
u
in this station, then this is true also under static priority cases because
G_{k}(u)=0
implies
F_{k}(u)=0
. Thus, from linear attraction for static priority policies, we know that the queues with
\epsilon_{i}>0
will decrease. (2) If there exists a
u
such that
G_{k}(u)\leq 0
, then for station
j
, from Lemma
6
,
u
is a convex combination of static priorities in this station. Thus,
u_{z,\epsilon}=\sum_{\pi^{\prime},z^{j}}\alpha_{\pi^{\prime},z}u_{\pi^{\prime},z^{j},z^{-j}}
. Since
G_{k}(u)
is a linear (in particular, convex) function of
u
, we have

\sum_{k\in\mathcal{H}_{\Delta}(z,\epsilon)}h_{k}G_{k}(u)=\sum_{\pi^{\prime},z}\alpha_{\pi^{\prime},z}\sum_{k\in\mathcal{H}_{\pi^{\prime}}:z_{k}>0}h_{k}G_{k}(u_{\pi^{\prime},z})\leq-r\text{.}

Note that the classes counted in
\{k\in\mathcal{H}_{\Delta}(z,\epsilon)\}
and
\{k\in\mathcal{H}_{\pi^{\prime}}:z_{k}>0\}
are the same. The reason is that the classes in station
j
all have
\epsilon_{i}=0
and do not appear in
\{k\in\mathcal{H}_{\Delta}(z,\epsilon)\}
; they also do not appear in
\{k\in\mathcal{H}_{\pi^{\prime}}:z_{k}>0\}
since we constructed the set so that
z_{k}=0
for all
k\in\mathcal{H}_{\pi^{\prime}}
.

This completes the proof of (
5.14
) and hence of state-space collapse under any queue-ratio discipline. ∎∎

6
Summary

What we put forward in this paper is a framework to study policy robustness—with regards to stability—in multiclass queueing networks, with the “uncertainty set” containing all queue-ratio policies.
The framework is a hierarchical one that breaks robust stability into two ingredients: (i) robust stability of the Skorohod problem (SP), and (ii) robust state-space collapse (SSC).

We prove that the stability under a policy within this uncertainty set is inherited from that of the “vertices” of the set; these “vertices” represent static-priority policies. In other words, in our framework, one must only verify the stability of the corner points of the uncertainty set to deduce policy robustness. Our methodology brings together techniques from optimization and applied probability to revisit and make progress on a fundamental question in stochastic modeling.

It is plausible that the specific implementation of each step in our framework can be strengthened. Our treatment of the Skorohod problem is relatively complete, and this is the focus of much of the theoretical development. There is likely room to strengthen the results pertaining to state-space collapse. Our requirement of linear attraction (with a common test vector
h
) is useful for tractability but is, admittedly, a strong one. It is this requirement that necessitates, in our example networks, the consideration of an arrival rate
\alpha
that is “large enough.” Going beyond linear attraction is a natural next step.

It is also plausible that one can go beyond queue-ratio policies to cover policies that have non-linear state-space collapse (i.e., where the matrix
\Delta
depends itself on the workload). We leave that, too, for future research.

Appendix A
Proofs of Supporting Lemmas

Proof.

Proof of Lemma
1
.

According to Definition
5
, except the completely-
\mathcal{S}
condition, for fixed partition
(a,b)
for
\mathcal{J}
, the Chen-
\mathcal{S}
condition is satisfied if the optimization model (
A.1
) has strictly negative optimal value.

\begin{split}\max_{h,v}\quad\quad&h_{a}^{\top}\theta_{a}+h_{a}^{\top}R_{ab}v\\
s.t.\quad&R_{b}v=-\theta_{b}\\
&h\in\mathbb{R}^{J}_{++},v\in\mathbb{R}^{|b|}_{+}\end{split}

(A.1)

(
A.1
) is equivalent to

\begin{split}{\max_{h\geq e}\quad h_{a}^{\top}\theta_{a}-\;\;\max_{\alpha^{b}\in\mathbb{R}^{|b|}}}\quad&\alpha^{b}\theta_{b}\\
s.t.\quad&{\alpha^{b}}^{\top}R_{b}\geq h_{a}^{\top}R_{ab}\end{split},

(A.2)

which is no larger than

\begin{split}\max_{h,\alpha^{b}}\quad\quad&h_{a}^{\top}\theta_{a}-\alpha_{b}^{\top}\theta_{b}\\
s.t.\quad&{\alpha^{b}}^{\top}R_{b}\geq h_{a}^{\top}R_{ab}\\
&\alpha^{b}\in\mathbb{R}^{|b|},h\geq e\end{split}.

(A.3)

Thus, we can rewrite (
A.3
) and add the completely-
\mathcal{S}
constraint, which gives an optimization model (
A.4
) that if (
A.4
) has strictly positive optimal value, then Chen-
\mathcal{S}
property holds for a fixed queue-ratio policy (so fixed
\Delta
), where
M
is a large enough positive number.

\begin{split}{\max_{h,\alpha^{b},\epsilon,\eta^{b}}}\quad\epsilon\\
s.t.\quad&h_{a}^{\top}\theta_{a}-\alpha^{b\top}\theta_{b}\leq-\epsilon\qquad\forall(a,b)\\
&h_{a}^{\top}R_{ab}\leq\alpha^{b\top}R_{b}\qquad\forall(a,b)\\
&\epsilon e\leq R_{b}\eta^{b}\qquad\forall b\subseteq J\\
&\alpha^{b}\in\mathbb{R}^{|b|},h\geq e,\epsilon\leq M,\eta^{b}\geq e^{b}\end{split}

(A.4)

As a result, the SP is robustly queue-ratio stable if
\rho<e
and the min-max problem

\begin{split}{\min_{\Delta\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{K\times J}:CM\Delta=I}\;\;\max_{h,\alpha^{b},\epsilon,\eta^{b}}}\quad\epsilon\\
s.t.\quad&h_{a}^{\top}\theta_{a}-\alpha^{b\top}\theta_{b}\leq-\epsilon\qquad\forall(a,b)\\
&h_{a}^{\top}R_{ab}\leq\alpha^{b\top}R_{b}\qquad\forall(a,b)\\
&\epsilon e\leq R_{b}\eta^{b}\qquad\forall b\subseteq J\\
&\alpha^{b}\in\mathbb{R}^{|b|},h\geq e,\epsilon\leq M,\eta^{b}\geq e^{b}\end{split}

(A.5)

has a strictly positive solution. ∎∎

Proof.

Proof of Lemma
2
.

This proof follows from that of Corollary 2.6 in
[
5
]
.
Since
R
is
Schur-S
all of its principal sub-matrices are non-singular, so that
R_{b}
is invertible. Thus,
\left\{v\in R_{+}^{|b|}:\theta_{b}+R_{b}v=0\right\}
contains the single vector
u=-R_{b}^{-1}\theta_{b}\in R_{+}^{|b|}
. As in the proof of Corollary 2.8 in
[
5
]
, if
R
is both
completely-
\mathcal{S}
and
Schur-S
, there exists a positive vector
h
such that
h_{a}^{{}^{\prime}}\left[\theta_{a}-R_{ab}R_{b}^{-1}\theta_{b}\right]<0
for any partition
(a,b)
of
\mathcal{J}
. This same
h
satisfies, in particular,

h_{a}^{{}^{\prime}}\left[\theta_{a}+R_{ab}u\right]<0

for any partition
(a,b)
of
\mathcal{J}
with
u=-R_{b}^{-1}\theta_{b}\in R_{+}^{|b|}
, and thus, all
u\in\left\{v\in R_{+}^{|b|}:\theta_{b}+R_{b}v=0\right\}
. The pair
(R,\theta)
is therefore Chen-
\mathcal{S}
. ∎∎

Proof.

Proof of Lemma
4
.

First, we prove that

R_{\Delta_{1}}
and
R_{\Delta_{2}}
are proportional in the
k^{th}
row with the ratio

(R_{\Delta_{1}})_{k.}=\frac{det(R_{\Delta_{2}}^{-1})}{det(R_{\Delta_{1}}^{-1})}(R_{\Delta_{2}})_{k.}.

(A.6)

Let
(R_{\Delta_{1}}^{-1})^{*}
and
(R_{\Delta_{2}}^{-1})^{*}
be the adjugate matrices of
R_{\Delta_{1}}^{-1}
and
R_{\Delta_{2}}^{-1}
respectively, then with the known fact,

R_{\Delta_{1}}=(R_{\Delta_{1}}^{-1})^{-1}=\frac{(R_{\Delta_{1}}^{-1})^{*}}{det(R_{\Delta_{1}}^{-1})},\;R_{\Delta_{2}}=(R_{\Delta_{2}}^{-1})^{-1}=\frac{(R_{\Delta_{2}}^{-1})^{*}}{det(R_{\Delta_{2}}^{-1})},

(A.7)

In our settings,
R_{\Delta_{1}}^{-1}
and
R_{\Delta_{2}}^{-1}
differ only in the
k^{th}
column, whose cofactor matrices
Co(R_{\Delta_{1}}^{-1})

and
Co(R_{\Delta_{2}}^{-1})
then have an identical
k^{th}
column,
and
(R_{\Delta_{1}}^{-1})^{*}=Co^{\top}(R_{\Delta_{1}}^{-1})
and
(R_{\Delta_{2}}^{-1})^{*}=Co^{\top}(R_{\Delta_{2}}^{-1})
have the same
k^{th}
row.

Thus, from (
A.7
), we have

\begin{split}(R_{\Delta_{1}})_{k.}&=\frac{(R_{\Delta_{1}}^{-1})^{*}_{k.}}{det(R_{\Delta_{1}}^{-1})}=\frac{(R_{\Delta_{2}}^{-1})^{*}_{k.}}{det(R_{\Delta_{1}}^{-1})}=\frac{det(R_{\Delta_{2}}^{-1})}{det(R_{\Delta_{1}}^{-1})}(R_{\Delta_{2}})_{k.},\end{split}

which implies that
R_{\Delta_{1}}
and
R_{\Delta_{2}}
are proportional in the
k^{th}
row with the ratio in (
A.6
).

Second, from (
5.4
), using the Sherman-Morrison formula, we can write

R_{\Delta_{3}}=(R_{\Delta_{1}}^{-1}+(1-\lambda)uv^{\top})^{-1}=R_{\Delta_{1}}-\frac{(1-\lambda)R_{\Delta_{1}}uv^{\top}R_{\Delta_{1}}}{1+(1-\lambda)v^{\top}R_{\Delta_{1}}u}.

Similarly, from (
5.5
),

R_{\Delta_{3}}=(R_{\Delta_{2}}^{-1}-\lambda uv^{\top})^{-1}=R_{\Delta_{2}}+\frac{\lambda R_{\Delta_{2}}uv^{\top}R_{\Delta_{2}}}{1-\lambda v^{\top}R_{\Delta_{2}}u}.

Suppose

A_{1}=\frac{uv^{\top}R_{\Delta_{1}}}{1+(1-\lambda)v^{\top}R_{\Delta_{1}}u},\;A_{2}=\frac{uv^{\top}R_{\Delta_{2}}}{1-\lambda v^{\top}R_{\Delta_{2}}u},

then we get
R_{\Delta_{3}}=R_{\Delta_{1}}(I-(1-\lambda)A_{1})=R_{\Delta_{2}}(I+\lambda A_{2})
.

Now, we prove that
A_{1}=A_{2}
. Let

E_{1}=\frac{R_{\Delta_{1}}}{1+(1-\lambda)v^{\top}R_{\Delta_{1}}u},E_{2}=\frac{R_{\Delta_{2}}}{1-\lambda v^{\top}R_{\Delta_{2}}u}.

Since only the
k^{th}
column of the matrix
uv^{\top}
is non-zero, if we prove that
E_{1}
and
E_{2}
have the same
k^{th}
row, then we can conclude that

A_{1}=uv^{\top}E_{1}=uv^{\top}E_{2}=A_{2}.

Using (
A.6
), to show that
E_{1}
and
E_{2}
have an identical
k^{th}
row, it suffices to show

\frac{1+(1-\lambda)v^{\top}R_{\Delta_{1}}u}{1-\lambda v^{\top}R_{\Delta_{2}}u}=\frac{det(R_{\Delta_{2}}^{-1})}{det(R_{\Delta_{1}}^{-1})}.

(A.8)

Define

B_{1}=v^{\top}R_{\Delta_{1}}=(R_{\Delta_{1}})_{k}.,\;B_{2}=v^{\top}R_{\Delta_{2}}=(R_{\Delta_{2}})_{k}.,\;w=\frac{det(R_{\Delta_{2}}^{-1})}{det(R_{\Delta_{1}}^{-1})}.

Then, we have
B_{1}=wB_{2}
and

\frac{1+(1-\lambda)v^{\top}R_{\Delta_{1}}u}{1-\lambda v^{\top}R_{\Delta_{2}}u}=\frac{1+(1-\lambda)B_{1}u}{1-\lambda B_{2}u}=\frac{1+(1-\lambda)wB_{2}u}{1-\lambda B_{2}u}.

Thus, to establish (
A.8
), it suffices to prove that

\frac{1+(1-\lambda)wB_{2}u}{1-\lambda B_{2}u}=w.

Namely, we have

\begin{split}&1+(1-\lambda)wB_{2}u=w-w\lambda B_{2}u\\
\Leftrightarrow\;&1+wB_{2}u-\lambda wB_{2}u=w-w\lambda B_{2}u\\
\Leftrightarrow\;&1+wB_{2}u=w\\
\Leftrightarrow\;&w(1-B_{2}u)=1\\
\Leftrightarrow\;&w(1-B_{2}u)B_{2}=B_{2}\\
\Leftrightarrow\;&B_{1}-B_{2}uB_{1}=B_{2}.\end{split}

Since
uv^{\top}=CMQ(\Delta_{2}-\Delta_{1})=R_{\Delta_{2}}^{-1}-R_{\Delta_{1}}^{-1}
, we have

u=(R_{\Delta_{2}}^{-1})_{.k}-(R_{\Delta_{1}}^{-1})_{.k}.

As

B_{1}(R_{\Delta_{1}}^{-1})_{.k}=1,\;B_{2}(R_{\Delta_{2}}^{-1})_{.k}=1,

we have

B_{2}uB_{1}=B_{2}((R_{\Delta_{2}}^{-1})_{.k}-(R_{\Delta_{1}}^{-1})_{.k})B_{1}=(1-B_{2}(R_{\Delta_{1}}^{-1})_{.k})B_{1}=B_{1}-B_{2}.

Overall, we have
B_{1}-B_{2}uB_{1}=B_{1}-(B_{1}-B_{2})=B_{2}
, and we can conclude that
A_{1}=A_{2}
. Define
A=A_{1}=A_{2}
, then
R_{\Delta_{3}}=R_{\Delta_{1}}(I-(1-\lambda)A)=R_{\Delta_{2}}(I+\lambda A)
, where
A=\displaystyle\frac{uv^{\top}R_{\Delta_{1}}}{1+(1-\lambda)v^{\top}R_{\Delta_{1}}u}=\frac{uv^{\top}R_{\Delta_{2}}}{1-\lambda v^{\top}R_{\Delta_{2}}u}
. ∎∎

Proof.

Proof of Lemma
3
.

We suppose that
R_{\Delta_{1}}^{-1}
and
R_{\Delta_{2}}^{-1}
are invertible. Using the Sherman-Morrison formula, we have that
R_{\Delta_{3}}^{-1}=R_{\Delta_{1}}^{-1}+(1-\lambda)uv^{\top}
is invertible if and only if

1+(1-\lambda)v^{\top}R_{\Delta_{1}}^{-1}u\neq 0.

Since
v^{\top}R_{\Delta_{1}}=(R_{\Delta_{1}})_{k.}=\displaystyle\frac{det(R_{\Delta_{2}}^{-1})}{det(R_{\Delta_{1}}^{-1})}(R_{\Delta_{2}})_{k.}
,
u=(R_{\Delta_{2}}^{-1})_{k.}-(R_{\Delta_{1}}^{-1})_{k.}
,

\begin{split}&1+(1-\lambda)v^{\top}R_{\Delta_{1}}^{-1}u\neq 0\\
\Leftrightarrow\;\;&1+(1-\lambda)(R_{\Delta_{1}})_{k.}((R_{\Delta_{2}}^{-1})_{k.}-(R_{\Delta_{1}}^{-1})_{k.})\neq 0\\
\Leftrightarrow\;\;&1+(1-\lambda)((R_{\Delta_{1}})_{k.}(R_{\Delta_{2}}^{-1})_{k.}-1)\neq 0\\
\Leftrightarrow\;\;&1+(1-\lambda)\left(\frac{det(R_{\Delta_{2}}^{-1})}{det(R_{\Delta_{1}}^{-1})}(R_{\Delta_{2}})_{k.}(R_{\Delta_{2}}^{-1})_{k.}-1\right)\neq 0\\
\Leftrightarrow\;\;&1+(1-\lambda)\left(\frac{det(R_{\Delta_{2}}^{-1})}{det(R_{\Delta_{1}}^{-1})}-1\right)\neq 0\\
\Leftrightarrow\;\;&\frac{det(R_{\Delta_{1}}^{-1})}{det(R_{\Delta_{2}}^{-1})}\neq 1-\frac{1}{\lambda}.\\
\end{split}

Since
\lambda\in(0,1)
, we have
1-\frac{1}{\lambda}\in(-\infty,0)
. Thus, if
det(R_{\Delta_{1}}^{-1})
and
det(R_{\Delta_{2}}^{-1})
have the same sign, then
R_{\Delta_{3}}^{-1}
is invertible.
∎∎

Proof.

Proof of Lemma
5
.

Fix a regular time
t
, so that
\dot{\overline{Z}}(t)
and
\dot{\overline{\epsilon}}(t)
exist. Let
j\in\mathcal{J}
be such that
\overline{\epsilon}_{k}(t)=0
for all
k\in\mathcal{C}(j)
. Suppose that there exists
k
such that
\dot{\epsilon}_{k}(t)<0
. For such
k
, it must be the case that
\overline{\epsilon}_{k}(t-)>0
and
\overline{\epsilon}_{k}(t+)<0
. Let
B:=\{k\in\mathcal{C}(j):\overline{\epsilon}_{k}(t-)\geq 0\}
. Then
\sum_{k\in B}u_{k}=1
at
t-
. However, since at
t+
, these are negative, it will be the case that
\sum_{k\in B}u_{k}=0
at
t+
so that
\dot{T}(t)
does not exist at
t
, which is a contradiction to its regularity. A similar argument can be applied if
\dot{\epsilon}_{k}(t)>0
. ∎∎

Proof.

Proof of Lemma
6
.

First, note that
F_{k}(u)=G_{k}(u)-\delta_{k}\sum_{l\in\mathcal{C}(j)}m_{l}G_{l}(u)
. Since
F_{k}(u)=0
, we have
G_{k}(u)=\delta_{k}\sum_{l\in\mathcal{C}(j)}m_{l}G_{l}(u)
. If
G_{k}(u)\leq 0
for some
k
with
\delta_{k}>0
, then it must be the case that
\sum_{l\in\mathcal{C}(j)}m_{l}G_{l}(u)\leq 0
. If there exists
l
with
G_{l}(u)>0
, then by the above we would have a
contradiction because
0<G_{l}(u)=\delta_{l}\sum_{l\in\mathcal{C}(j)}m_{l}G_{l}(u)\leq 0
.

Second, recall that
F(u)=(I-\Delta CM)G(u)
. We can consider the polyhedron of values
y\in\mathbb{R}^{|\mathcal{C}(j)|}
such that
y_{l}-\delta_{l}\sum_{l\in\mathcal{C}(j)}m_{i}y_{i}=0
for all
l\in C(j)
and
y_{l}\leq 0
for all
l\in\mathcal{C}(j)
. The extreme points of this polyhedron have
y_{l}=0
for all but one class. Any
point in this polyhedron is a convex combination of these extreme points. Take the true allocation
G_{k}(u)
in this station. Then, the above says that
G_{k}(u)
is a convex combination of these extreme points and since
G_{k}(u)
is a linear function and
(I-P^{\prime})
is invertible, it must be the case that
u
is a convex combination of
u_{k}
that solve
G_{k}(u)=0
for all
k\in\mathcal{C}(j)
but one. ∎∎

Appendix B
Detailed Proofs for Balanced DHV Networks

B.1
Chen-
\mathcal{S}
in Balanced DHV Networks

In this appendix, we prove that the pairs
(R,\theta)
for the
8
static-priority policies in a balanced DHV network (
m_{1}+m_{4}=1
;
m_{2}+m_{5}=1
;
m_{3}+m_{6}=1
) with
\alpha_{1}<1
, are all Chen-
\mathcal{S}
with invertible reflection matrices and same-sign determinants. In turn, Theorem
4
allows us to say that the refection matrix is invertible and Chen-
\mathcal{S}
for any ratio matrix
\Delta
.

For each of the 8 static priority policy, to prove that the corresponding
(R,\theta)
is Chen-
\mathcal{S}
, we must show that
R
is completely-
\mathcal{S}
, and consider all the 7 partitions of
\mathcal{J}
and show that there exists a positive vector
h\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
such that (
2.8
) is satisfied for all the 7 partitions.

Proof.

Proof for priority 2-4-6 (Chen-S).

In the balanced DHV network under priority 2-4-6, we have
J=3,K=6,\rho=\left[\alpha_{1}\ \alpha_{1}\ \alpha_{1}\right]^{\prime},

R=\frac{1}{m_{1}m_{3}m_{5}+m_{2}m_{4}m_{6}}\left[\begin{matrix}m_{1}m_{3}m_{5}&m_{1}m_{4}m_{6}&-m_{1}m_{4}m_{5}\\
-m_{2}m_{5}&m_{1}m_{5}&m_{5}(m_{2}-m_{1})\\
m_{3}(m_{2}-m_{3})&-m_{3}m_{6}&m_{3}m_{5}\end{matrix}\right].

Remind that a matrix is completely-
\mathcal{S}
if all of its principal submatrices are
\mathcal{S}
-matrices; and a square matrix
P
is called an
\mathcal{S}
-matrix if there exists a positive vector
u
such that
Pu>0
. Thus, to prove that the reflection matrix
R
is completely-
\mathcal{S}
, we need to prove that all its submatrices, say matrix
P
, are
\mathcal{S}
-matrices.

(i)

All the three
1\times 1
principle submatrices are
\mathcal{S}
-matrices because all of
R
’s diagonal elements are positive, and there obviously exists a positive vector
u
such that
Pu>0
;

(ii)

All the
2\times 2
principle submatrices are
\mathcal{S}
-matrices:

When
P=\displaystyle\frac{1}{m_{1}m_{3}m_{5}+m_{2}m_{4}m_{6}}\left[\begin{matrix}m_{1}m_{3}m_{5}&m_{1}m_{4}m_{6}\\
-m_{2}m_{5}&m_{1}m_{5}\\
\end{matrix}\right]
, it is easy to find a
u>0
with
u_{2}
being large enough such that
Pu>0
as required.

When
P=\displaystyle\frac{1}{m_{1}m_{3}m_{5}+m_{2}m_{4}m_{6}}\left[\begin{matrix}m_{1}m_{3}m_{5}&-m_{1}m_{4}m_{5}\\
m_{3}(m_{2}-m_{3})&m_{3}m_{5}\\
\end{matrix}\right]
, if
m_{2}-m_{3}\geq 0
, then it is easy to find a
u>0
with
u_{1}
being large enough such that
Pu>0
as required. If
m_{2}-m_{3}<0
, suppose
Pu=k>0
,
u=P^{-1}k
. Then
P^{-1}>0
is sufficient to show that
P
is a
\mathcal{S}
-matrix.
P^{-1}>0
is equivalent to
m_{1}m_{3}^{2}m_{5}^{2}+m_{1}m_{4}m_{5}m_{3}(m_{2}-m_{3})>0\Leftrightarrow m_{1}m_{3}m_{5}(m_{3}m_{5}+m_{4}(m_{2}-m_{3}))>0
. Since
m_{3}m_{5}+m_{4}(m_{2}-m_{3})\geq m_{3}m_{5}+m_{2}-m_{3}=m_{2}-m_{3}m_{2}=m_{2}m_{6}>0
, we conclude that
P^{-1}>0
, and
P
is a
\mathcal{S}
-matrix.

When
P=\displaystyle\frac{1}{m_{1}m_{3}m_{5}+m_{2}m_{4}m_{6}}\left[\begin{matrix}m_{1}m_{5}&m_{5}(m_{2}-m_{1})\\
-m_{3}m_{6}&m_{3}m_{5}\\
\end{matrix}\right]
, if
m_{2}-m_{1}\geq 0
, then it is easy to find a
u>0
with
u_{1}
being large enough such that
Pu>0
as required. If
m_{2}-m_{1}<0
, similar as above,
P^{-1}>0
is sufficient to show that
P
is a
\mathcal{S}
-matrix.
P^{-1}>0
is equivalent to
m_{1}m_{3}m_{5}^{2}+m_{3}m_{6}m_{5}(m_{2}-m_{1})>0\Leftrightarrow m_{3}m_{5}(m_{1}m_{5}+m_{6}(m_{2}-m_{1}))>0
. Since
m_{1}m_{5}+m_{6}(m_{2}-m_{1})\geq m_{1}m_{5}+m_{2}-m_{1}=m_{2}-m_{1}m_{2}=m_{2}m_{4}>0
, we conclude that
P^{-1}>0
, and
P
is a
\mathcal{S}
-matrix.

(iii)

The
3\times 3
principle submatrix, which is
R
, is a
\mathcal{S}
-matrices since
R^{-1}=CMQ\Delta>0
.

Thus, we can conclude that the reflection matrix
R
is completely-
\mathcal{S}
.

Let
B=m_{1}m_{3}m_{5}+m_{2}m_{4}m_{6}>0
, then

R=\frac{1}{B}\left[\begin{matrix}m_{1}m_{3}m_{5}&m_{1}m_{4}m_{6}&-m_{1}m_{4}m_{5}\\
-m_{2}m_{5}&m_{1}m_{5}&m_{5}(m_{2}-m_{1})\\
m_{3}(m_{2}-m_{3})&-m_{3}m_{6}&m_{3}m_{5}\end{matrix}\right],

\displaystyle\theta=R(\rho-e)

\displaystyle=\frac{\alpha_{1}-1}{B}\left[\begin{matrix}m_{1}m_{3}m_{5}+m_{1}m_{4}m_{6}-m_{1}m_{4}m_{5}\\
m_{1}m_{5}-m_{2}m_{5}+m_{5}m_{2}-m_{5}m_{1}\\
m_{3}m_{2}-m_{3}m_{3}-m_{3}m_{6}+m_{3}m_{5}\end{matrix}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{\alpha_{1}-1}{B}\left[\begin{matrix}m_{1}m_{3}m_{5}+m_{1}m_{4}m_{6}-m_{1}m_{4}m_{5}\\
0\\
0\end{matrix}\right].

Let
A=(\alpha_{1}-1)(m_{1}m_{3}m_{5}+m_{1}m_{4}m_{6}-m_{1}m_{4}m_{5})
. Since

\begin{split}m_{1}m_{3}m_{5}+m_{1}m_{4}m_{6}-m_{1}m_{4}m_{5}&=m_{1}(m_{3}m_{5}+m_{4}m_{6}-m_{4}m_{5})\\
&=m_{1}(m_{3}m_{5}+m_{4}(m_{6}-m_{5})),\end{split}

A=(\alpha_{1}-1)m_{1}(m_{3}m_{5}+m_{4}(m_{6}-m_{5})).

If
m_{6}-m_{5}\geq 0
, we have

m_{3}m_{5}+m_{4}(m_{6}-m_{5})>0;

if
m_{6}-m_{5}<0
, we have

\begin{split}m_{3}m_{5}+m_{4}(m_{6}-m_{5})>\;m_{3}m_{5}+(m_{6}-m_{5})=\;m_{6}-m_{5}(1-m_{3})=\;m_{6}-m_{5}m_{6}=m_{2}m_{6}>0.\end{split}

Thus, we have
m_{3}m_{5}+m_{4}(m_{6}-m_{5})>0
, and obviously, with
\alpha_{1}<1
, we have
A<0
,
so that

\displaystyle\theta=\left[\begin{matrix}\displaystyle\frac{A}{B}\\
0\\
0\end{matrix}\right]\leq 0.

Partition 1:
a=1;b=2,3

\theta_{b}=\theta_{23}=\left[\begin{matrix}0\\
0\end{matrix}\right]\Rightarrow u=-R_{b}^{-1}\theta_{b}=\left[\begin{matrix}0\\
0\end{matrix}\right].

Thus,
h_{a}^{{}^{\prime}}\left[\theta_{a}+R_{ab}u\right]=h_{1}\left[\theta_{1}+0\right]=h_{1}\theta_{1}=h_{1}\displaystyle\frac{A}{B}
. Since
A<0,B>0
, taking
h_{1}>0
, we ensure
h_{a}^{{}^{\prime}}\left[\theta_{a}+R_{ab}u\right]<0
.

Partition 2:
a=2;b=1,3

\theta_{b}=\theta_{13}=\left[\begin{matrix}\displaystyle\frac{A}{B}\mcr 0\end{matrix}\right],\;R_{b}^{-1}=R_{13}^{-1}=\left[\begin{matrix}\displaystyle\frac{1}{m_{1}}&\displaystyle\frac{m_{4}}{m_{3}}\mcr-\displaystyle\frac{m_{2}m_{6}-m_{3}m_{5}}{m_{1}m_{5}}&1\end{matrix}\right]=\left[\begin{matrix}\displaystyle\frac{1}{m_{1}}&\displaystyle\frac{m_{4}}{m_{3}}\mcr\displaystyle\frac{m_{3}-m_{2}}{m_{1}m_{5}}&1\end{matrix}\right]

\Rightarrow u=-R_{b}^{-1}\theta_{b}=\frac{A}{B}\left[\begin{matrix}-\displaystyle\frac{1}{m_{1}}\mcr\displaystyle\frac{m_{2}-m_{3}}{m_{1}m_{5}}\end{matrix}\right];R_{ab}=\frac{1}{B}\left[\begin{matrix}-m_{2}m_{5}&m_{5}(m_{2}-m_{1})\end{matrix}\right];\theta_{a}=\theta_{2}=0.

Thus,

\displaystyle h_{a}^{{}^{\prime}}\left[\theta_{a}+R_{ab}u\right]

\displaystyle=h_{2}\left[0+\frac{m_{2}m_{5}A}{B^{2}m_{1}}+\frac{Am_{5}(m_{2}-m_{1})(m_{2}-m_{3})}{B^{2}m_{1}m_{5}}\right]

\displaystyle=h_{2}\frac{A}{B^{2}}\left[\frac{m_{2}m_{5}}{m_{1}}+\frac{(m_{2}-m_{1})(m_{2}-m_{3})}{m_{1}}\right]

\displaystyle=h_{2}\frac{A}{B^{2}m_{1}}\left[m_{2}m_{5}+(m_{2}-m_{1})(m_{2}-m_{3})\right].

We have

\begin{split}&m_{2}m_{5}+(m_{2}-m_{1})(m_{2}-m_{3})=\;m_{2}m_{5}+m_{2}m_{2}-m_{2}m_{3}-m_{2}m_{1}+m_{1}m_{3}\\
=\;&m_{2}-m_{2}m_{3}-m_{2}m_{1}+m_{1}m_{3}=\;m_{2}m_{6}+m_{1}m_{3}-m_{2}m_{1}=\;m_{2}m_{6}+m_{1}(m_{3}-m_{2}),\end{split}

if
m_{3}-m_{2}\geq 0
, then

m_{2}m_{6}+m_{1}(m_{3}-m_{2})>0;

if
m_{3}-m_{2}<0
, then

\begin{split}&m_{2}m_{6}+m_{1}(m_{3}-m_{2})>\;m_{2}m_{6}+(m_{3}-m_{2})=\;m_{3}-m_{2}(1-m_{6})=\;m_{3}-m_{2}m_{3}=m_{5}m_{3}>0.\end{split}

Thus, we get
m_{2}m_{5}+(m_{2}-m_{1})(m_{2}-m_{3})>0
.
Since we also have
A<0,B>0,m_{1}>0
, taking
h_{2}>0
, we ensure
h_{a}^{{}^{\prime}}\left[\theta_{a}+R_{ab}u\right]<0
.

Partition 3:
a=3;b=1,2

\theta_{b}=\theta_{12}=\left[\begin{matrix}\displaystyle\frac{A}{B}\mcr 0\end{matrix}\right],\;R_{b}^{-1}=R_{12}^{-1}=\left[\begin{matrix}1&-\displaystyle\frac{m_{4}m_{6}}{m_{3}m_{5}+m_{5}m_{6}}\mcr\displaystyle\frac{m_{2}}{m_{1}}&m_{3}\end{matrix}\right]=\left[\begin{matrix}1&-\displaystyle\frac{m_{4}m_{6}}{m_{5}}\mcr\displaystyle\frac{m_{2}}{m_{1}}&m_{3}\end{matrix}\right]

\Rightarrow u=-R_{b}^{-1}\theta_{b}=-\frac{A}{B}\left[\begin{matrix}1\mcr\displaystyle\frac{m_{2}}{m_{1}}\end{matrix}\right];R_{ab}=\frac{1}{B}\left[\begin{matrix}m_{3}(m_{2}-m_{3})&-m_{3}m_{6}\end{matrix}\right];\theta_{a}=\theta_{3}=0.

Thus,

\displaystyle h_{a}^{{}^{\prime}}\left[\theta_{a}+R_{ab}u\right]

\displaystyle=h_{3}\left[0+\frac{m_{3}(m_{3}-m_{2})A}{B^{2}}+\frac{Am_{3}m_{6}m_{2}}{B^{2}m_{1}}\right]

\displaystyle=h_{3}\frac{A}{B^{2}}m_{3}\left[m_{3}-m_{2}+\frac{m_{6}m_{2}}{m_{1}}\right]

\displaystyle=h_{3}\frac{A}{B^{2}}\frac{m_{3}}{m_{1}}\left[m_{1}(m_{3}-m_{2})+m_{6}m_{2}\right].

Since
m_{1}(m_{3}-m_{2})+m_{6}m_{2}>0
(we already get this when we check
A<0
),
A<0,B>0,\displaystyle\frac{m_{3}}{m_{1}}>0
, taking
h_{3}>0
, we ensure
h_{a}^{{}^{\prime}}\left[\theta_{a}+R_{ab}u\right]<0
.

Partition 4:
a=1,2;b=3

\theta_{b}=\theta_{3}=0;R_{b}^{-1}=R_{3}^{-1}\;{\rm exists}\Rightarrow u=-R_{b}^{-1}\theta_{b}=0;

R_{ab}=\frac{1}{B}\left[\begin{matrix}-m_{1}m_{4}m_{5}\mcr m_{5}(m_{2}-m_{1})\end{matrix}\right]\Rightarrow R_{ab}u=\left[\begin{matrix}0\mcr 0\end{matrix}\right];\theta_{a}=\theta_{12}=\left[\begin{matrix}\displaystyle\frac{A}{B}\mcr 0\end{matrix}\right]

Thus,

\displaystyle h_{a}^{{}^{\prime}}\left[\theta_{a}+R_{ab}u\right]

\displaystyle=\left[\begin{matrix}h_{1}&h_{2}\end{matrix}\right]\left[\begin{matrix}\displaystyle\frac{A}{B}\mcr 0\end{matrix}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{h_{1}A}{B}

Since
A<0,B>0
, taking
h_{1}>0
, we ensure
h_{a}^{{}^{\prime}}\left[\theta_{a}+R_{ab}u\right]<0
.

Partition 5:
a=1,3;b=2

\theta_{b}=\theta_{2}=0;R_{b}^{-1}=R_{2}^{-1}\;{\rm exists}\Rightarrow u=-R_{b}^{-1}\theta_{b}=0;

R_{ab}=\frac{1}{B}\left[\begin{matrix}m_{1}m_{4}m_{6}\mcr-m_{3}m_{6}\end{matrix}\right]\Rightarrow R_{ab}u=\left[\begin{matrix}0\mcr 0\end{matrix}\right];\theta_{a}=\theta_{13}=\left[\begin{matrix}\displaystyle\frac{A}{B}\mcr 0\end{matrix}\right]

Thus,

\displaystyle h_{a}^{{}^{\prime}}\left[\theta_{a}+R_{ab}u\right]

\displaystyle=\left[\begin{matrix}h_{1}&h_{3}\end{matrix}\right]\left[\begin{matrix}\displaystyle\frac{A}{B}\mcr 0\end{matrix}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{h_{1}A}{B}

Since
A<0,B>0
, taking
h_{1}>0
, we ensure
h_{a}^{{}^{\prime}}\left[\theta_{a}+R_{ab}u\right]<0
.

Partition 6:
a=2,3;b=1

\theta_{b}=\theta_{1}=\frac{A}{B};\;R_{b}^{-1}=R_{1}^{-1}=\frac{m_{1}m_{3}m_{5}+m_{2}m_{4}m_{6}}{m_{1}m_{3}m_{5}}=1+\frac{m_{2}m_{4}m_{6}}{m_{1}m_{3}m_{5}}

\Rightarrow u=-R_{b}^{-1}\theta_{b}=-(1+\frac{m_{2}m_{4}m_{6}}{m_{1}m_{3}m_{5}})\frac{A}{B};\;R_{ab}=\frac{1}{B}\left[\begin{matrix}-m_{2}m_{5}\mcr m_{3}(m_{2}-m_{3})\end{matrix}\right];\;\theta_{a}=\theta_{23}=\left[\begin{matrix}0\mcr 0\end{matrix}\right]

Thus,

\displaystyle h_{a}^{{}^{\prime}}\left[\theta_{a}+R_{ab}u\right]

\displaystyle=\left[\begin{matrix}h_{2}&h_{3}\end{matrix}\right]\left[\begin{matrix}\displaystyle\frac{A}{B^{2}}m_{2}m_{5}(1+\frac{m_{2}m_{4}m_{6}}{m_{1}m_{3}m_{5}})\mcr\displaystyle\frac{A}{B^{2}}m_{3}(m_{3}-m_{2})(1+\frac{m_{2}m_{4}m_{6}}{m_{1}m_{3}m_{5}})\end{matrix}\right]\mcr

\displaystyle=\frac{A}{B^{2}}(1+\frac{m_{2}m_{4}m_{6}}{m_{1}m_{3}m_{5}})\left[m_{2}m_{5}h_{2}+m_{3}(m_{3}-m_{2})h_{3}\right]

Since
A<0,B>0,(1+\displaystyle\frac{m_{2}m_{4}m_{6}}{m_{1}m_{3}m_{5}})>0
, fixing
h_{2}>0
large enough and
h_{3}>0
small enough, we ensure
h_{a}^{{}^{\prime}}\left[\theta_{a}+R_{ab}u\right]<0
.

Partition 7:
a=1,2,3;b=\varnothing

h_{a}^{{}^{\prime}}\left[\theta_{a}+R_{ab}u\right]=h^{\prime}\theta=h_{1}\frac{A}{B}

Since
A<0,B>0,h_{1}>0
, we ensure
h_{a}^{{}^{\prime}}\left[\theta_{a}+R_{ab}u\right]<0
.

We have shown, then, that exists a positive vector
h\in{R^{3}}
with
h_{2}
being a large enough positive number, and
h_{3}
being a small enough positive number as required so that
(R,\theta)
under the static-priority policy 2-4-6 is Chen-
\mathcal{S}
.
∎

Proof.

Proof for priority 1-5-3 (Chen-S).

In the balanced DHV network under priority 1-5-3, we have
J=3,K=6,\rho=\left[\alpha_{1}\ \alpha_{1}\ \alpha_{1}\right]^{\prime},

R=\left[\begin{matrix}\displaystyle\frac{1}{m_{2}}&-\displaystyle\frac{m_{4}}{m_{2}}&0\mcr-\displaystyle\frac{m_{5}}{m_{4}}&1&0\mcr\displaystyle\frac{m_{3}-m_{2}}{m_{2}m_{4}}&-\displaystyle\frac{m_{3}}{m_{2}}&1\end{matrix}\right],

\displaystyle\theta=R(\rho-e)

\displaystyle=(\alpha_{1}-1)\left[\begin{matrix}\displaystyle\frac{m_{1}}{m_{2}}\mcr 1-\displaystyle\frac{m_{5}}{m_{4}}\mcr\displaystyle\frac{m_{1}}{m_{2}m_{4}}(m_{3}-m_{2})\end{matrix}\right]\mcr

\displaystyle=\frac{\alpha_{1}-1}{m_{2}m_{4}}\left[\begin{matrix}m_{1}m_{4}\mcr m_{2}m_{4}-m_{2}m_{5}\mcr m_{1}m_{3}-m_{1}m_{2}\end{matrix}\right].

Similar to the priority 2-4-6 case, it can be proved that the reflection matrix
R
is completely-
\mathcal{S}
, we omit the details here.

Partition 1:
a=1;b=2,3

\theta_{b}=\theta_{23}=\frac{\alpha_{1}-1}{m_{2}m_{4}}\left[\begin{matrix}m_{2}m_{4}-m_{2}m_{5}\mcr m_{1}m_{3}-m_{1}m_{2}\end{matrix}\right],\;R_{b}^{-1}=R_{23}^{-1}=\left[\begin{matrix}1&0\mcr\displaystyle\frac{m_{3}}{m_{2}}&1\end{matrix}\right]\Rightarrow u=-R_{b}^{-1}\theta_{b}=\displaystyle\frac{1-\alpha_{1}}{m_{4}}\left[\begin{matrix}m_{4}-m_{5}\mcr m_{3}-m_{1}\end{matrix}\right];

R_{ab}=\left[\begin{matrix}-\displaystyle\frac{m_{4}}{m_{2}}&0\end{matrix}\right]\Rightarrow R_{ab}u=(\alpha_{1}-1)(1-\frac{m_{1}}{m_{2}}).

Thus,
h_{a}^{{}^{\prime}}\left[\theta_{a}+R_{ab}u\right]=h_{1}(\alpha_{1}-1)
. Since
\alpha_{1}<1
, taking
h_{1}>0
, we ensure
h_{a}^{{}^{\prime}}\left[\theta_{a}+R_{ab}u\right]<0
.

Partition 2:
a=2;b=1,3

\theta_{b}=\theta_{13}=\frac{\alpha_{1}-1}{m_{2}m_{4}}\left[\begin{matrix}m_{1}m_{4}\mcr m_{1}m_{3}-m_{1}m_{2}\end{matrix}\right],\;R_{b}^{-1}=R_{13}^{-1}=\left[\begin{matrix}m_{2}&0\mcr\displaystyle\frac{m_{2}-m_{3}}{m_{4}}&1\end{matrix}\right]\Rightarrow u=-R_{b}^{-1}\theta_{b}=\left[\begin{matrix}(1-\alpha_{1})m_{1}\mcr 0\end{matrix}\right];

R_{ab}=\left[\begin{matrix}-\displaystyle\frac{m_{5}}{m_{4}}&0\end{matrix}\right]\Rightarrow R_{ab}u=(\alpha_{1}-1)\frac{m_{1}m_{5}}{m_{4}}.

Thus,
h_{a}^{{}^{\prime}}\left[\theta_{a}+R_{ab}u\right]=(\alpha_{1}-1)h_{2}(\displaystyle\frac{m_{1}}{m_{2}}+\displaystyle\frac{m_{1}m_{5}}{m_{4}})
. Since
\alpha_{1}<1
, taking
h_{2}>0
, we ensure
h_{a}^{{}^{\prime}}\left[\theta_{a}+R_{ab}u\right]<0
.

Partition 3:
a=3;b=1,2

\theta_{b}=\theta_{12}=\frac{\alpha_{1}-1}{m_{2}m_{4}}\left[\begin{matrix}m_{1}m_{4}\mcr m_{2}m_{4}-m_{2}m_{5}\end{matrix}\right],\;R_{b}^{-1}=R_{12}^{-1}=\left[\begin{matrix}1&\displaystyle\frac{m_{4}}{m_{2}}\mcr\displaystyle\frac{m_{5}}{m_{4}}&\displaystyle\frac{1}{m_{2}}\end{matrix}\right]\Rightarrow u=-R_{b}^{-1}\theta_{b}=\left[\begin{matrix}1-\alpha_{1}\mcr 1-\alpha_{1}\end{matrix}\right];

R_{ab}=\left[\begin{matrix}\displaystyle\frac{m_{3}-m_{2}}{m_{2}m_{4}}&-\displaystyle\frac{m_{3}}{m_{2}}\end{matrix}\right]\Rightarrow R_{ab}u=(1-\alpha_{1})\frac{m_{3}m_{1}-m_{2}}{m_{2}m_{4}}.

Thus,
h_{a}^{{}^{\prime}}\left[\theta_{a}+R_{ab}u\right]=h_{3}(\alpha_{1}-1)
. Since
\alpha_{1}<1
, taking
h_{3}>0
, we ensure
h_{a}^{{}^{\prime}}\left[\theta_{a}+R_{ab}u\right]<0
.

Partition 4:
a=1,2;b=3

\theta_{b}=\theta_{3}=\frac{\alpha_{1}-1}{m_{2}m_{4}}(m_{1}m_{3}-m_{1}m_{2})\;,R_{b}^{-1}=R_{3}^{-1}=1\;\Rightarrow u=-R_{b}^{-1}\theta_{b}=\frac{1-\alpha_{1}}{m_{2}m_{4}}(m_{1}m_{3}-m_{1}m_{2});

R_{ab}=\left[\begin{matrix}0\mcr 0\end{matrix}\right]\Rightarrow R_{ab}u=\left[\begin{matrix}0\mcr 0\end{matrix}\right];\theta_{a}=\theta_{12}=\frac{\alpha_{1}-1}{m_{2}m_{4}}\left[\begin{matrix}m_{1}m_{4}\mcr m_{2}m_{4}-m_{2}m_{5}\end{matrix}\right]

Thus,

\displaystyle h_{a}^{{}^{\prime}}\left[\theta_{a}+R_{ab}u\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{\alpha_{1}-1}{m_{2}m_{4}}\left[\begin{matrix}h_{1}&h_{2}\end{matrix}\right]\left[\begin{matrix}m_{1}m_{4}\mcr m_{2}m_{4}-m_{2}m_{5}\end{matrix}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{\alpha_{1}-1}{m_{2}m_{4}}\left[m_{1}m_{4}h_{1}+m_{2}(m_{4}-m_{5})h_{2}\right]

Since
\alpha_{1}<1
, fixing
h_{1}>0
large enough and
h_{2}>0
small enough, we ensure
h_{a}^{{}^{\prime}}\left[\theta_{a}+R_{ab}u\right]<0
.

Partition 5:
a=1,3;b=2

\theta_{b}=\theta_{2}=(\alpha_{1}-1)(1-\frac{m_{5}}{m_{4}})\;,R_{b}^{-1}=R_{2}^{-1}=1\;\Rightarrow u=-R_{b}^{-1}\theta_{b}=(1-\alpha_{1})(1-\frac{m_{5}}{m_{4}});

R_{ab}=\left[\begin{matrix}-\displaystyle\frac{m_{4}}{m_{2}}\mcr-\displaystyle\frac{m_{3}}{m_{2}}\end{matrix}\right]\Rightarrow R_{ab}u=\frac{\alpha_{1}-1}{m_{2}m_{4}}\left[\begin{matrix}m_{4}(m_{4}-m_{5})\mcr m_{3}(m_{4}-m_{5})\end{matrix}\right];\theta_{a}=\theta_{13}=\frac{\alpha_{1}-1}{m_{2}m_{4}}\left[\begin{matrix}m_{1}m_{4}\mcr m_{1}m_{3}-m_{1}m_{2}\end{matrix}\right]

\displaystyle=(\alpha_{1}-1)\left[h_{1}+\frac{m_{3}-m_{1}}{m_{4}}h_{3}\right]

Since
\alpha_{1}<1
, fixing
h_{1}>0
large enough and
h_{3}>0
small enough, we ensure
h_{a}^{{}^{\prime}}\left[\theta_{a}+R_{ab}u\right]<0
.

Partition 6:
a=2,3;b=1

\theta_{b}=\theta_{1}=(\alpha_{1}-1)\frac{m_{1}}{m_{2}}\;,R_{b}^{-1}=R_{1}^{-1}=m_{2}\;\Rightarrow u=-R_{b}^{-1}\theta_{b}=(1-\alpha_{1})m_{1};

R_{ab}=\left[\begin{matrix}-\displaystyle\frac{m_{5}}{m_{4}}\mcr\displaystyle\frac{m_{3}-m_{2}}{m_{2}m_{4}}\end{matrix}\right]\Rightarrow R_{ab}u=\frac{\alpha_{1}-1}{m_{2}m_{4}}\left[\begin{matrix}m_{1}m_{2}m_{5}\mcr m_{2}m_{1}-m_{3}m_{1}\end{matrix}\right];\theta_{a}=\theta_{23}=\frac{\alpha_{1}-1}{m_{2}m_{4}}\left[\begin{matrix}m_{2}m_{4}-m_{2}m_{5}\mcr m_{1}m_{3}-m_{1}m_{2}\end{matrix}\right]

Thus,

\displaystyle h_{a}^{{}^{\prime}}\left[\theta_{a}+R_{ab}u\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{\alpha_{1}-1}{m_{4}}\left[\begin{matrix}h_{2}&h_{3}\end{matrix}\right]\left[\begin{matrix}m_{4}-m_{5}+m_{1}m_{5}\mcr 0\end{matrix}\right]

\displaystyle=(\alpha_{1}-1)m_{2}h_{2}

Since
\alpha_{1}<1
, taking
h_{2}>0
, we ensure
h_{a}^{{}^{\prime}}\left[\theta_{a}+R_{ab}u\right]<0
.

Partition 7:
a=1,2,3;b=\varnothing

h_{a}^{{}^{\prime}}\left[\theta_{a}+R_{ab}u\right]=h^{\prime}\theta=\frac{\alpha_{1}-1}{m_{2}m_{4}}\left[m_{1}m_{4}h_{1}+m_{2}(m_{4}-m_{5})h_{2}+m_{1}(m_{3}-m_{2})h_{3})\right]

Since
\alpha_{1}<1
, fixing
h_{1}>0
large enough,
h_{2}>0
and
h_{3}>0
small enough, we ensure
h_{a}^{{}^{\prime}}\left[\theta_{a}+R_{ab}u\right]<0
.

We have shown, then, that exists a positive vector
h\in{R^{3}}
with
h_{1}
being a large enough positive number,
h_{2}
and
h_{3}

(R,\theta)

\mathcal{S}

\displaystyle=(\alpha_{1}-1)\left[\begin{matrix}\displaystyle\frac{m_{1}}{m_{3}}\mcr\displaystyle\frac{m_{4}-m_{5}}{m_{4}}\mcr\displaystyle\frac{m_{2}m_{4}-m_{3}m_{4}+m_{3}-m_{2}}{m_{2}m_{4}}\end{matrix}\right]\mcr

\displaystyle=(\alpha_{1}-1)\left[\begin{matrix}\displaystyle\frac{m_{1}}{m_{3}}\mcr\displaystyle\frac{m_{4}-m_{5}}{m_{4}}\mcr\displaystyle\frac{m_{1}(m_{3}-m_{2})}{m_{2}m_{4}}\end{matrix}\right].

Similar to the priority 2-4-6 case, it can be proved that the reflection matrix
R
is completely-
\mathcal{S}
, we omit the details here.

, fixing

large enough and

Proof.

Proof for priority 4-5-6 (Chen-S).

In the balanced DHV network under priority 4-5-6, we have
J=3,K=6,\rho=\left[\alpha_{1}\ \alpha_{1}\ \alpha_{1}\right]^{\prime},

R=\left[\begin{matrix}1&0&-m_{4}\mcr-\displaystyle\frac{m_{2}}{m_{1}}&1&\displaystyle\frac{m_{2}}{m_{1}}-1\mcr 0&-\displaystyle\frac{m_{3}}{m_{2}}&\displaystyle\frac{m_{3}}{m_{2}}\end{matrix}\right],

\displaystyle\theta=R(\rho-e)

\displaystyle=(\alpha_{1}-1)\left[\begin{matrix}1-m_{4}\mcr 1-\displaystyle\frac{m_{2}}{m_{1}}+\displaystyle\frac{m_{2}}{m_{1}}-1\mcr\displaystyle\frac{m_{3}}{m_{2}}-\displaystyle\frac{m_{3}}{m_{2}}\end{matrix}\right]\mcr

\displaystyle=(\alpha_{1}-1)\left[\begin{matrix}m_{1}\mcr 0\mcr 0\end{matrix}\right].

Similar to the priority 2-4-6 case, it can be proved that the reflection matrix
R

\mathcal{S}

Partition 1:

Proof.

Proof for priority 1-2-6 (Chen-S).

In the balanced DHV network under priority 1-2-6, we have

R=\left[\begin{matrix}1&\displaystyle\frac{m_{4}m_{6}}{m_{3}m_{5}}&-\displaystyle\frac{m_{4}}{m_{3}}\mcr-\displaystyle\frac{m_{5}}{m_{4}}&1&0\mcr 0&-\displaystyle\frac{m_{6}}{m_{5}}&1\end{matrix}\right],

\displaystyle\theta=R(\rho-e)

\displaystyle=(\alpha_{1}-1)\left[\begin{matrix}1+\displaystyle\frac{m_{4}m_{6}}{m_{3}m_{5}}-\displaystyle\frac{m_{4}}{m_{3}}\mcr 1-\displaystyle\frac{m_{5}}{m_{4}}\mcr 1-\displaystyle\frac{m_{6}}{m_{5}}\end{matrix}\right]\mcr

\displaystyle=\frac{\alpha_{1}-1}{m_{3}m_{4}m_{5}}\left[\begin{matrix}m_{3}m_{4}m_{5}+m_{4}^{2}m_{6}-m_{4}^{2}m_{5}\mcr m_{3}m_{5}(m_{4}-m_{5})\mcr m_{3}m_{4}(m_{5}-m_{6})\end{matrix}\right].

is completely-
\mathcal{S}
, we omit the details here.

Partition 1:

Thus, we have
K>0
. Since
\alpha_{1}<1
, fixing
h_{1}>0
large enough,
h_{2}>0
and
h_{3}>0
small enough, we ensure

(R,\theta)
under the static-priority policy 1-2-6 is Chen-
\mathcal{S}

Lemma 3.1) A nonsingular M-matrix is both completely-
